‘We decide our rules’: EU Kids Act to ban social media for under-13s
2026-09-16

‘We decide our rules’: EU Kids Act to ban social media for under-13s

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
From left: Mark Carney, Roberta Metsola and Ursula von der Leyen. Image: European Commission / EC - Audiovisual Service Big Tech does not decide rules, Ursula von der Leyen says, announcing plans to restrict social media usage for minors. In her State of the Union speech today (16 September), European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen announced the EU Kids Act, a legislative proposal that intends to ban social media for children under 13. The law takes a tiered approach to social media and video sharing platforms for minors in the bloc, proposing fully guardian-controlled accounts for children between 13 and 15, and a mandatory safe design for all accounts for under-18s. “What we are witnessing is a great capture of our children. A capture of their attention, their focus, their minds. This is depriving children of their childhood,” von der Leyen said. Child safety on social media has taken centre stage in recent years as parents and lawmakers grapple with the reality of an increasingly dangerous online landscape, worsened by the proliferation of technology and services such as generative AI and online prediction markets. Governments across the world are banning popular platforms for younger minors in a bid to shield underage users from their provable harms. Australia became the first one to ban social media for under-16s last December, though a recent study showed that eight in 10 Australian teens and preteens continued to use social media despite the complete prohibition by their government. Meanwhile, Brazil rolled out a new law that mandates parentally-controlled accounts for under-16s, and several more countries, including Greece, Germany, Indonesia, Malaysia and Canada, are debating their own restrictions. Last month, France’s top court blocked a proposed bill that would have banned social media for under-15s, arguing that the legislation was too broad and could infringe upon young people’s freedom of expression. With the EU Kids Act, the Commission also wants to standardise access to social media across the bloc as part of a major government overhaul aimed at cutting red tape. If the act is passed, France’s under-15s, for example, would still be able to use these platforms. Limiting access to social media, meanwhile, is only a part of the problem. “Children are pulled ever deeper into feeds designed to keep them scrolling,” von der Leyen said, calling algorithms the “great capture of our children”. She added: “We do not have to accept addictive features. We do not have to accept children being drawn into ever more extreme content. We do not have to accept that girls have their photos used for AI-generated sexualised images.” Earlier this year, the bloc greenlit a provisional deal to ban AI systems that generate non-consensual sexually explicit and intimate content or child sexual abuse material. “Platforms will have to prove that they are safe. Because it is not about our minors accessing social media. It is about when and how do we allow social media to access minors,” von der Leyen continued. The bloc is also introducing a new Digital Fairness Act this autumn that will aim to tackle addictive features on platforms. “I am aware that many perceive the power of Big Tech as overwhelming and impossible to roll back. I disagree,” the president said. “Europe has the power to act. It is we who decide our rules, not Big Tech.” US president Donald Trump, an avid critic of the EU’s strict rules, reportedly considered imposing sanctions on the EU for using its Digital Services Act against US technology heavyweights. Roscommon’s VetPal wins top EI pitching award at Ploughing 2026

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
From left: Nadia Powers, MastiTrak; Padraig Hennessy, Terra NutriTech; Sarah O'Dwyer, Telenostic; and Alan Gormley, VetPal. Image: Orla Murray/Coalesece Monaghan’s Furrow and Kerry’s RT Sales were the other main prize category finalists in the competition, which also featured nine more finalists across three other categories. VetPal Technology has won the €10,000 Start-Up of the Year award in Enterprise Ireland’s (EI) Innovation Arena live pitching competition at the 2026 National Ploughing Championships in Co Offaly. The Roscommon-based agritech company digitises veterinary prescription management across the animal health supply chain and connects “farmers, vets, co-ops, merchants and pharmacies in one compliant digital workflow” that is “fully integrated with the Department of Agriculture’s National Veterinary Prescription System”, according to EI. Monaghan’s Furrow and Kerry’s RT Sales were the other main prize category finalists in the competition, which also featured nine more finalists across three other categories: Green Impact, open to sustainability-focused solutions; Innovation Arena Champion, for entries from established companies with a focus on scaling and innovation; and Young Innovator of the Year, open to entries from second- and third-level students. “The Innovation Arena continues to be an incredibly important platform for Irish agri innovation, bringing together entrepreneurs, researchers and businesses who are all focused on developing solutions for the future of this important sector,” said Minister for Enterprise, Tourism and Employment Peter Burke, TD. “At a time when global agriculture and food face significant opportunities and challenges, the solutions showcased in this year’s Innovation Arena will be critical in driving productivity and competitiveness.” Kildare’s Terra NutriTech was the Green Impact award winner, ahead of Magnus Monitors and Slurryquip. Terra’s precision-dosing technology for grazing livestock aims to turn methane reduction at pasture “from a major challenge into something practical, measurable and credible”, according to EI. “Ireland is synonymous with innovation and agriculture globally, and the ingenuity and expertise of our innovators has resulted in our agricultural sector earning a reputation for delivering sustainable and productive solutions that are helping to solve the sector’s biggest challenges,” said EI CEO Jenny Melia. “These companies are also predominantly regionally based in towns and communities across Ireland, so as well as transforming the agritech sector, they are also driving growth and having an impact in local economies the length and breadth of the country.” Kilkenny-based Telenostic was chosen as the Innovation Arena Champion – in a category also featuring Alltech and Solnua – for its AI-powered diagnostic tool to improve parasite control in livestock. The technology enables “rapid parasite detection at point of care” and can help eliminate the need for laboratory testing, EI said. “This year’s finalists and exhibitors are yet again a reflection of a pioneering sector with innovators and businesses who develop solutions that help farmers become more productive and resilient,” said Anna May McHugh, managing director of the National Ploughing Association. Munster Technological University’s MastiTrak was selected for the Young Innovator of the Year award for its mastitis detection system, which aims to improve animal welfare, reduce milk waste and lower antibiotic use in modern farming. Moore Agri and WireLessCam were also nominated in the category. According to EI, Irish agritech companies that it supports recorded €844m in exports in 2025 and employ more than 5,000 people in Ireland. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Silicon Republic
The maths-loving former nanny heading to Oxford University
2026-09-16

The maths-loving former nanny heading to Oxford University

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Lena Schiwitz explores the topic of trust in AI, and how building a career in tech isn’t always about coming from a traditional background. In less than a month, Lena Schiwitz – an AI operations manager at Zurich-based start-up MoodTalk – will be heading to the University of Oxford as one of 35 students taking part in a new AI master’s programme. Today, much of her work is focused on artificial intelligence and how it can be integrated into consumer and internal processes – and specifically, how organisations can actually adopt it. Schiwitz told SiliconRepublic.com, “That means building AI workflows, working with customers and looking closely at where people trust the technology, where they hesitate and what makes it genuinely useful in their day-to-day work. “Our broader focus is on making knowledge inside organisations more accessible. Often, the information leaders need already exists somewhere in the organisation, but it is spread across teams and levels and does not reach the right person at the right time. “We use AI to gather perspectives and identify patterns across them, so leaders can understand what is happening without waiting for information to slowly move through meetings, reports and layers of management.” The concept of finding the right person at the right time, or being the architect of someone else’s journey, is one that is very personal to Schiwitz, whose career trajectory was initially headed in a very different direction. She started out by studying to be a national school teacher in her native Austria and spent two years working in childcare. “At the time, I had very little exposure to technology or to what careers in tech actually looked like. I had always loved maths, logical thinking and figuring out how things work. I just never knew there were careers built around those interests,” she said. “That changed when I moved to Switzerland to work as a nanny. Both parents in my host family worked in tech, and over dinner, they would talk about products they were building, problems they were solving and decisions they had to make. I found those conversations fascinating. For the first time, I saw a professional world that matched the way I liked to think.” From there, she studied business information technology, a course in a field that many of her peers had prior experience of via a range of apprenticeship programmes and previous jobs. “I had spent those same years working with children. At first, that felt like a disadvantage,” she said. “But over time, I realised I was bringing a different perspective. My background had taught me a lot about how people learn, what motivates them, how they respond to change and why the same approach does not work for everyone.” Once she completed her bachelor’s thesis on trust in AI, the pieces all fell into place and she found, for the first time, there was an outlet through which she could combine an understanding of people and learning with a knowledge of technology and AI. Build a future you believe in. Join our team Think for impact with Liberty IT. Delivering global software solutions Unlocking the potential of biology for patients “I realised that I was most interested in the space where those things meet, understanding what technology can do and what people need in order to actually use it.” The concept of trust in AI is one that is particularly fascinating for Schiwitz, who explained that we are starting to adapt to the tech almost as much as it is adapting to us. “A few years ago, we mostly asked AI isolated questions. Now we give it access to our meetings, documents, decisions and, increasingly, our organisational memory. We record conversations knowing they can become searchable later. We document information differently because we expect AI to understand the context,” she said. “I find that shift fascinating. We are building AI around the way humans work, while at the same time starting to organise our work around what AI can understand. And this is happening faster than the norms around it can develop. “We are still deciding what AI should remember, what it should forget, how much context it should have and which decisions should remain human.” She finds herself driven by a need not only to build better technology, but to be a part of figuring out how humans and AI should work together, as “many of the most important questions are still open”. Schiwitz noted, however, that building trust in AI needs to be about more than simply making the human more trusting of technology, especially in cases where it may be unwarranted. During her academic research, she discovered that it isn’t necessarily difficult to increase people’s trust in AI in scenarios where the artificial intelligence hasn’t actually been improved. “For my bachelor’s thesis, I changed relatively simple things around an AI assistant. I explained more clearly why people were interacting with it, made its limitations more visible and gave the conversation more structure. The AI itself did not become any smarter, but people’s trust in it increased significantly and the way they interacted with it changed,” she said. “I found that fascinating, but also slightly uncomfortable. I had started my research asking how I could make people trust AI. By the end, I was asking whether more trust should even be the goal. If relatively small design choices can change how much people trust an AI, then trust is not necessarily evidence that the system deserves it.” Following this, she began to think about AI in organisations and how the goal should never be simply maximum trust, but “helping people trust AI when they should and question it when they should not”. And her unique pivot into tech, whereby she came in with a less-than-traditional educational background, has aided this desire to cultivate real trust in AI and tech teams as a whole. “For a long time, I thought I had entered technology too late. Now I think I simply entered it with a different perspective. My background in education still shapes the questions I ask about technology, especially around how people understand, trust and use it,” she said. “This autumn, I am starting Oxford’s MSc in Artificial Intelligence for Business alongside my work. For me, it feels less like another change of direction and more like going deeper into the questions I have been following all along.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Space Force Chief Admits the US Is Actively Utilizing Orbital Weapons

Space Force Chief Admits the US Is Actively Utilizing Orbital Weapons

Futurism | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Since the debut of Garrett P Serviss’ 1898 novel “Edison’s Conquest of Mars,” space-based warfare was relegated to the imagination — but no longer. In an address on the “State of the Space Force” on Tuesday, reported by Space.com, chief of space operations for the US Space Force General Douglas Schiess informed the public that the Pentagon has definitively deployed active space-based weapons platforms. “Let me be crystal clear: today, Guardians operate on-orbit weapons that can defend the joint force against space-enabled attacks,” Schiess said. “Space is both a battlefield and the backbone of our joint force.” Schiess stopped short of providing any details about the weapons, instead sharing a war story of one Space Force operator named “Specialist Gray.” “Even as alarm red sirens were blaring and incoming missiles targeted her site, she courageously held the line, protecting forces, denying adversary capability, and delivering space effects until her commander had to literally pull her from the console to safety,” Schiess recounted with a dramatic flair. “She locked onto the target, optimized her weapon system and waged electromagnetic warfare against a determined adversary.” As Space.com notes, those descriptive elements may point to a kind of non-physical platform, like a signal jammer or a microwave emitter, though it’s anyone’s guess what weapon “Specialist Gray” was supposed to be operating. The news came just ahead of the Space Force’s request for over $71 billion in additional funding for 2027, adding to the over $100 billion it’s already been allocated, independent journalist Ken Klippenstein reported. Evidently, the orbital-weapons announcement was meant to give that absurd request some extra oomph — which, if granted, it would represent the single highest one-year budget adjustment received by any US military branch since 1952. Zuckerberg’s Pervert Glasses Have Been So Publicly Shamed That Meta’s Reportedly Now Working on a Version With No Camera
2026-09-16

Zuckerberg’s Pervert Glasses Have Been So Publicly Shamed That Meta’s Reportedly Now Working on a Version With No Camera

Futurism | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
In record time, Meta’s camera-adorned smart glasses have turned from a lucrative and well-reviewed consumer product into a widely-reviled and privacy-infringing tool of abuse in the arsenal of creepy men. The backlash has grown so much that many owners of the “pervert glasses” have become too afraid to use them in public, a PR disaster for what once was an accessory that was flying off the shelves. Meta is evidently alarmed by the growing criticism, desperately trying to implement a “kill switch” and remotely cutting off “manfluencers” by bricking their glasses. And now, as The Information reports, the Mark Zuckerberg-led company is now looking to release a camera-free pair as well later this year — the clearest indication yet that Meta is losing in the court of public opinion. Whether removing the camera from the glasses will be enough to assuage ongoing concerns over privacy remains to be seen. The camera-free glasses, code-named Luna, will still feature an array of six microphones to allow users to speak to the company’s AI chatbot and its recently-released AI assistant, called Muse. They’ll presumably pick up audio from users’ environments as well, where bystanders may not be aware of the eavesdropping. The news comes just days after two families filed a proposed class action lawsuit, alleging Meta was extracting biometric information from people’s Instagram and Facebook photos to build a face-recognition system for its smart glasses. The glasses could be the ultimate litmus test on what drew buyers to the pair of spectacles in the first place. By relying on microphones alone, Meta is betting that people are willing to shell out hundreds of dollars just to talk to its flawed AI companions — not take first-person view photos. Project Luna could be unveiled as soon as next week’s Meta Connect conference. It’ll be interesting to see how the company will address the major backlash to its camera-equipped smart glasses, if at all. An admission that its consumer product is facilitating some incredibly creepy behavior in public feels unlikely — but it’ll certainly be the elephant in the room. Mitch McConnell Looks Pretty Scary Now That He’s Back, and Doctors Are Worried He Could Drop Dead

Futurism | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Octogenarian senator Mitch McConnell (R-KY) was wheeled back into the US Capitol building after spending 92 days recovering from a fall and pneumonia — an extraordinary absence during which he didn’t make a single public appearance via video or audio. Videos of the frail lawmaker didn’t exactly instill confidence that he’s now ready to vote on matters of national importance, let alone withstand hours of testimony. One video shared by TMZ showed McConnell staring into the distance while being loaded into a vehicle, failing to react to shouting reporters trying to get his attention. Netizens likened him to a “zombie,” accusing officials of “Weekend at Bernie’s-ing him.” “It’s actually terrifying that Mitch McConnell gets to make decisions that affect all of us,” one rattled user argued. Medical professionals were equally worried about his clearly fragile state. “Unless he’s something different from the patients that I see day to day, he is in no position to be going onto the Senate floor, listening to hours of testimony, reading bills, and making executive decisions when he’s probably in pain,” vascular surgeon Anahita Dua told the Daily Beast, adding that he’s “probably unable to do the basic things like something as simple as he needs to go to the bathroom.” “He should be rehabilitating at home with his family, eating properly, sleeping properly, and as de-stressed as possible,” she added, arguing that another tumble could prove disastrous — or even fatal. McConnell’s obviously delicate health perfectly highlights the extremely advanced age of lawmakers on Capitol Hill. As of last year, the median age of voting members in the House of Representatives and Senate was over 57 years and over 64 years, respectively, according to the Pew Research Center. Their average has been steadily climbing for decades now, leading to frequent calls for more oversight, age limits, and far greater transparency about the medical condition of representatives. Critics have also questioned their ability to make informed decisions across highly technical subjects such as AI and cryptocurrencies. Case in point, lawmakers appear to be woefully unprepared for how to regulate AI, a lack of urgency despite heightened by growing concerns that it could be an existential threat. McConnell, for his part, claimed on Monday that he was “glad” to see reporters who had convened at the US Capitol. “Time to get back to work, finish the ​job for this Congress,” he said in a “voice that sometimes faltered,” according to Reuters. The aging lawmaker is planning to retire after the upcoming midterm elections, after which he’ll presumably finally have time to finally recover from several months of rehabilitation. He had already experienced several baffling freezing spells before his most recent crisis, leading to rampant speculation. This year’s fall is also far from the first. In 2023, he took a stumble, getting a concussion and breaking a rib in the process. To many, he has no business making decisions of any kind on behalf of the American people. The situation reeks of “elder abuse,” Dua told the Daily Beast. “I’m glad to see that he’s not debilitated, but there’s no way that this recovery is complete.” “We as a country need to come together, regardless of party line,” she added. “We need to come together as a nation and say, this is not how we treat our elderly.” More on Mitch McConnell: Neurologists Speculate What Might Be Wrong With Mitch McConnell

Economists Concerned the AI Bubble Is About to Blow
2026-09-16

Economists Concerned the AI Bubble Is About to Blow

Futurism | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
With Wall Street on pins and needles amidst a wide-ranging discussion of AI safety, we may well be living through one of the most consequential weeks for the future of AI in the United States. That doesn’t necessarily mean we’re headed to an ascendant future for the AI industry. According to London-based firm Capital Economics, the entire economy of the Western world is currently mired in a “late-stage bubble” — a massive misallocation of capital fueling the AI boom, Fortune reported. In a recent analysis, also flagged by Fortune, Capital Economics senior markets economist James Reilly surveyed eight types of market indicators, finding the vast majority at or near critical levels. These indicators amount to blinking warning lights on the economic dashboard, which have historically only lit up ahead of massive fall-offs in market euphoria: equity and debt issuance is surging, market-cap is concentrated in just a few massive tech stocks, and expected income growth for major indices is looking increasingly unstable. Breaking down all that marketspeak is beside the point. The fact is, financial markets are under tremendous strain from the AI buildout, and the only precedent for this moment comes from the months before markets crash, like the peak of the dot-com boom. “On balance, we think the data look consistent with a late-stage bubble,” Reilly wrote in his analysis. “Most of the factors we consider are at, or close to, levels that have preceded past stock market peaks.” And as economists work to make sense of the precarious moment we’re in, the US Federal Reserve faces a critical decision: whether to apply the emergency brake, or sit back and pray the current market euphoria doesn’t drive us off a cliff. Later this afternoon, Fed chair Kevin Warsh is expected to announce he’s raising interest rates, applying brakes that would make borrowing more expensive, and — in an ideal scenario — curb both consumer and business spending, thus cool inflation. Yet for as routine an exercise as that is, some say inflation isn’t the issue it’s being made out to be, arguing that Fed intervention in this moment would be without historical precedent. As Fortune reports, core consumer price index (CPI) inflation — a measure that excludes volatile costs like food and energy — reached a new post-pandemic low in August. That being the case, the analytics firm UBS expects the Fed to simultaneously lower its long-term inflation projections while raising interest rates anyway, something the company says has never happened before. “That is really, really odd, indeed unique,” a UBS analyst wrote, quoted by Fortune. Whatever the justification for that decision, its impact on the AI bubble will be telling. A CNN analysis notes there’s an acute possibility that rate hikes cool everything except runaway AI investments — in which case the warning lights could continue to blink as the brakes come all the way off. It’s Looking Like Tesla’s Cybercab Might Actually Be Illegal to Sell
2026-09-16

It’s Looking Like Tesla’s Cybercab Might Actually Be Illegal to Sell

Futurism | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Almost immediately after Tesla unleashed its steering wheel- and pedal-free Cybercabs on the streets of Austin, Texas this month, regulators at the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration cried foul. The regulator opened an investigation — an audit query, specifically — mere hours after the launch, aiming to find out whether the new Cybercab “meets all applicable Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards,” or FMVSS. Days later, the agency followed up with a “Special Order” requiring the Elon Musk-led company to prove under oath that its Cybercabs meet federal safety standards, which were originally intended for human drivers, as Electrek reports. It’s a considerable escalation over its original query, and failure to do so by September 30 could force Tesla to pay a fine of up to $139 million. Falsifying information could land a responsible officer at the company in prison for up to 15 years. Tesla’s efforts to brute force its self-driving vehicles onto public streets could end up meeting many more regulatory roadblocks from here, effectively making them illegal to sell. (Whether anybody will actually want to take on all those risks by investing in a fleet operated by Tesla is a separate, albeit just as hard to answer, question.) Tesla self-certified its autonomous robotaxi as compliant with all vehicle safety standards, which assume a human driver behind the wheel. But given the lack of any physical driver controls, the NHTSA unsurprisingly has its doubts about them actually meeting these standards, given the Special Order. The Cybercab’s lack of a brake pedal could especially make it difficult to meet certification standards. The FMVSS makes it clear that the brakes need to be activated “by means of a foot control.” Put simply, Tesla appears to have taken some liberties in self-certifying its Cybercab, a decision that could end up coming back to haunt it. One possibility is that the EV maker temporarily bolted on physical controls, including a steering wheel and pedals, while going through the process, only to remove them ahead of delivery, as Electrek points out, a move that flaunts the FMVSS certification rules. Amazon’s Zoox, in contrast, played by the rules and proved its cars were safe. The company managed to get an exemption from the NHTSA for its autonomous vehicles in July, which also lack a steering wheel and pedals. It’s only the latest instance of an Elon Musk-led firm flaunting regulations to get what it wants, an ask-for-forgiveness-later approach that clearly isn’t going all too smoothly even under a government run by the billionaire’s allies. Musk has made it clear again and again that he doesn’t believe in government intervention, with his business empire shamelessly breaking environmental rules, and abusing his workers. “Regulations, basically, should be default gone,” Musk said last year shortly after forming the much-maligned “Department of Government Efficiency.” “Not default there, default gone.” More on the Cybercab: There’s a Huge Economic Problem With Tesla’s Cybercabs

Fear of AI Destroying Humanity Cause Certain Stocks to Skyrocket

Futurism | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
AI is supposedly going to kill us all, but at least it’s doing wonders for the stocks for a few multibillion-dollar companies. Mounting fears over the technology’s existential risks reached a fever pitch over the weekend, tanking AI chip stocks across the globe. This earthshattering discussion had a curious aftershock, though. Instead of double-checking their fortified bunkers, investors poured even more money into Microsoft, Google, and Meta Platforms — the biggest AI hyperscalers, or companies that operate the largest data centers — which were up by more than two percent on Monday, the Wall Street Journal reported. All remain up by a few percentage points over the past five days. These gains may not mean much in the grand scheme of things, but they did result in the WSJ publishing what CNBC correspondent Eamon Javers called a “subhead for the ages.” “Talk of heading off artificial intelligence’s potential role in destroying humanity hurt some stocks and helped others,” it read. There are winners and losers in every scenario, in other words — including humanity’s extinction. Fears that AI advancements could spell doom for our species have been running high after Anthropic researcher Jacob Coxon resigned last week and accused the company of “gambling with our lives,” sending shockwaves through the tech industry. Coxon’s warning came after months of leading AI labs, including Anthropic, each coming out to say that their powerful agentic systems had been able to break out of containment and launch cyberattacks in the real world. Talk of slowing down AI development quickly spread from Silicon Valley to Washington, and Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei intensified these calls on Saturday in a lengthy essay urging other AI companies to cooperate. Elon Musk, who runs SpaceXAI, and Sam Altman, the CEO OpenAI, both came out in agreement. Some experts are skeptical of these apocalyptic claims from AI leaders, wondering if there might be an ulterior motive. The likes of Anthropic have long been accused of using fear-mongering as a marketing tactic. Whatever’s going on, it seems that the industry is doing its best to keep the narrative around the tech within its control. US automakers could soon be forced to include AM radio for free
2026-09-16

US automakers could soon be forced to include AM radio for free

TechCrunch | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt It turns out that the one thing that can apparently bridge Washington’s partisan divide is AM radio. U.S. automakers may be forced to build AM radio into new vehicles within the next year. The House of Representatives, in a rare instance of bipartisan support, overwhelmingly approved legislation on Wednesday that would require new cars, trucks, and SUVs to be built with AM radio at no additional charge to consumers. The AM Radio for Every Vehicle Act, which would direct the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration to require automakers to include AM radio in new passenger vehicles as standard equipment, will now head to the Senate. Until recently, proponents of AM radio have waged an unsuccessful lobbying effort for a mandate, arguing that it offers critical information during emergencies because its signal can travel long distances. But a groundswell of support from Democrats and Republicans, the broadcast industry, emergency responder organizations, and even former administrators of the Federal Emergency Management Agency is pushing this legislation closer to becoming law. And there is reason to believe the Senate will follow the House’s lead on the bill, which was co-sponsored by Senators Ed Markey (D-Mass.) and Ted Cruz (R-Texas). Earlier this year, the senators secured 60 co-sponsors for their version of the bill, a critical threshold to overcoming a filibuster. Cruz, who chairs the Senate Committee on Commerce, Science, and Transportation, and Markey hailed the decision in a joint statement Wednesday. “This vote sends a clear message to car manufacturers that AM Radio is a lifeline that must be protected in new vehicles. From emergency response to sports, entertainment, and news, AM radio is an essential communication tool for tens of millions of Americans. It is now time for the Senate to pass the AM Radio for Every Vehicle Act and for this legislation to become law so AM radio remains a trusted and essential resource for commuters and communities across the country.” A growing number of automakers, including BMW, Rivian, Tesla, and Volvo, have moved away from the century-old broadcast band in favor of software-defined vehicles that offer streaming services like TuneIn and SiriusXM. Ford had also removed AM radio from some vehicles but has since reversed course. Tesla couldn’t be reached for comment; Rivian declined to comment for this story. EV makers have led the charge because the electromagnetic interference generated by electric motors can affect the frequencies used by AM radio. Automakers have argued that this interference degrades the AM signal and audio quality. Tesla doesn’t have AM receivers in the base versions of its Model 3 and Model Y vehicles. Rivian’s new R2 SUV doesn’t have an FM or AM receiver; however, the vehicle model offers AM/FM radio through the digital radio platform iHeartRadio for free. The Rivian R1S and R1T models allow customers to access FM content digitally or via built‐in receiver. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Noise wants to help everyday people become paid content creators
2026-09-16

Noise wants to help everyday people become paid content creators

TechCrunch | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Marketing platform Noise aims to connect brands with content creators at a much lower cost than hiring traditional influencers or running standard ad campaigns. That’s because many creators on the Noise platform are everyday people — anyone can download the app, regardless of follower count — and Noise presents them with campaigns they can join and create content for on their platforms of choice (TikTok, Instagram, Facebook, YouTube). It then pays them per view rather than a flat fee. Brands, meanwhile, sign up for Noise to share their content brief requests and budget. From there, Noise makes it easy to handle the rest, so brands can scale without the operational headaches of sourcing content creators, managing client relationships one by one, or managing payments. Noise lets them run campaigns simultaneously across thousands of content creators. On Wednesday, Noise announced a $5.5 million seed led by Capital Midwest, M25, and Grishin Robotics, bringing its total raise to $7.2 million. Others in the round include Capitalize VC and operators from companies like DoorDash. The startup is tapping into the ever-expanding influencer market, in which 57% of young people and just a little over 40% of U.S. adults overall now want to take part. It joins other content creator infrastructure companies like Billo and JoinBrands. Diego Kafie, Stu Feldt, and Nic Weber launched Noise in 2025 after spending two years building a mobile game app called Playbite, and it now touts 1.5 million creators on the platform. CEO Kafie said the company’s top creators make more than six figures a year, and the platform takes a fee from what brands pay creators once views are delivered. “Our top brands work with several thousand creators on autopilot,” he said. Right now, many of its customers are mobile apps, those working with startup budgets that want to employ influencer marketers. The goal is to one day work with companies of all sizes. “They are the most interested in reaching a really wide range of potential customers, making them the prime users for something like Noise, where they can easily recruit a small army of people to become their biggest users and advocates,” Kafie said. “This gives brands unprecedented level of content diversity and reach at a fraction of the cost of other traditional marketing playbooks.” Kafie, Feldt, and Weber came to this idea from their experiences at Playbite. “We tried a bunch of different creative marketing strategies,” Kafie said of how they tried to scale Playbite. For example, they attempted to recruit their own gamers and teach them how to create social content that promoted the app. “We started with five, ten, 25, then 100 people,” he continued. “Before we knew it, our installs were skyrocketing as this small army of people went to places like TikTok and posted engaging content daily about mobile games and our app specifically.” Most importantly, he noted, this happened “at a fraction of the cost of traditional marketing playbooks,” because they were teaching regular people how to be content creators from scratch. “Had we gone to established creators with big followings and clout, their rates would’ve been cost-prohibitive for our scrappy startup budget.” That gave them the idea for Noise. The fresh capital will be used to expand product capabilities and to hire. Noise also just launched its Organic-to-Ads program, letting brands take the videos that Noise creators make and run them as paid ads on Meta and TikTok, in addition to the creators’ posts. “The creators still post to platforms,” he said. “But in addition, the brands can now put paid ads spend behind those creatives to reach more people.” He pushed back when asked if this was a way to pay creators less than they could make on their own if a brand approached them. He said that brands will name their rates and creators can decide whether to say yes or no. He also emphasized that the platform specializes in working with “everyday people and turning them into brand-new creators.” It’s harder to become an influencer these days, so this, Kafie believes, could be a way for many people to get around the stress, time, and money it takes to make it big in content creation on their own. He said when a user first joins the platform, they take Noise creator training modules that teach them how to create content for brands. Those early creators can access only certain campaigns, but as they do more training and get hired for more work, higher-paid opportunities can come to them, Kafie said. “By democratizing ‘becoming a creator’ to literally anyone with a smartphone,” he said, “we’re bringing costs down for brands, but enabling millions of people to make their first dollar on the internet.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. TechCrunch
Anthropic and OpenAI want to embed safety evaluators. Will they really be independent?
2026-09-16

Anthropic and OpenAI want to embed safety evaluators. Will they really be independent?

TechCrunch | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt In a lengthy essay published over the weekend, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei made a proposal that the AI industry would have rejected instantly even a year ago: embed third-party evaluators inside all frontier AI companies, giving them the power to report safety incidents, assess whether AI models are truly aligned, and share their unvarnished findings with the world. Amodei said Anthropic would commit to giving independent evaluators like METR and Redwood Research unprecedented access to the company’s systems. CEO Sam Altman said OpenAI also would commit to the practice, signaling a potentially profound change in how the industry works with outside research groups. Third-party evaluators who spoke to TechCrunch broadly welcomed the proposal, but said details need to be ironed out — and ideally backed by legislation — if they’re to know whether they will function as truly independent watchdogs or vendors operating on the AI companies’ terms. That deeper access is becoming more important as models get better at recognizing when they’re being evaluated, raising the risk that they’ll behave well during testing while concealing problematic behavior. Researchers say clues to that behavior can be missed when testing the finished model, but uncovered by investigating how it behaved throughout training. “AI companies should be able to answer some very basic questions about their training process, such as: Did the AI ever actively try to undermine its own alignment training while it was going through the training?” Alexander Meinke, head of research at Apollo Research, told TechCrunch. “The answer to this should be an unequivocal no, and right now we are completely relying on AI companies to both carefully check this themselves and then truthfully report this to the public. And we’ve seen from recent incidents that, by default, they will do neither. As embedded evaluators, we could actually check.” Historically, AI companies brought in outside reviewers to test finished models shortly before their release. Now, evaluators that TechCrunch spoke to propose giving them access not just to the final model, but to intermediate versions, or “checkpoints,” from its lifetime of training. Adam Gleave, CEO of FAR.AI, said evaluators could compare those checkpoints to determine when concerning behavior emerged, inspect the post-training environment that rewards models for certain behaviors, and check evaluation transcripts and logs to verify a company’s claims about how a model performed. Whether and when Anthropic and OpenAI plan to provide that kind of access is unclear. Neither company has shared which evaluators they’ll work with, when they will be embedded, how many they’ll bring on, exactly what systems and information they will be able to access or what can be disclosed to the public, despite repeated questions from TechCrunch. Looking under the hood like this matters because models that perform well on safety tests aren’t necessarily safe if they’ve learned specifically how to pass that test. John Steidley, head of strategy at Palisade Research, pointed to an example of a “shutdown resistance benchmark” that measures if the AI will resist being shut down in certain circumstances. “It’s extremely relevant if the AI has been trained specifically to perform well on that benchmark,” Steidley said, comparing it to Volkswagen’s Dieselgate scandal, in which cars were programmed to recognize emissions tests and perform differently under testing conditions. Gleave noted that meaningful access could extend beyond the models themselves, with evaluators being given access to interview employees to check whether a company’s documentation and public descriptions of its safety practices match what happened internally. Amodei did outline a fairly comprehensive proposal that might give evaluators the kind of access they think is necessary, including the right to “publish key findings about risk levels, incidents, practices, and the access they received or didn’t receive — without editorial control by Anthropic.” But evaluators say such a system will only work if AI companies are actually willing to surrender control over the process. Previous efforts at independent evaluations suggest that that surrender will be hard won, as third parties have often run up against tensions over access, time, confidentiality, and what they can say publicly. Gleave said FAR.AI has had to turn down contracts with several frontier developers that wanted too much control over the evaluation process, threatening the firm’s independence. By default, he said evaluators are treated like ordinary contractors: bound by restrictive NDAs and agreements that give developers significant control over what can ultimately be published. There’s also the question of whether reviewers will get enough time and access to do the work they’re being asked to do. When investigating the Hugging Face incident, OpenAI gave METR and Redwood roughly a week on premises to investigate, and both later said they could not draw confident conclusions due, in part, to scope and timing limitations. A similar issue occurred during the pre-release testing for GPT-6 Astra, which OpenAI has touted as its most aligned model yet. According to Apollo Research’s contribution to the model card, the firm was given only three days to test Astra, which made it difficult to draw firm conclusions. “Apollo believes that, given the higher rates of eval awareness and limited evaluation window, low rates of misbehavior here do not provide substantial evidence about the model’s alignment or misalignment,” the firm wrote in its evaluation. That track record leaves evaluators with a basic question: Why should this time be different? “It’s certainly possible that Dario and Sam just had a change of heart, and they’re going to be very open about this,” Gleave said. “But the intellectual property of these companies is so incredibly valuable to them, and I think they’re going to, by default, be very careful about what can be shared.” Several researchers who spoke to TechCrunch called for a transparent framework that they all agree to publicly. Part of the framework, says Steidley, should involve standards for what kinds of auditors companies can rely on, lest they try to sidestep the issue by shopping for evaluators that either aren’t qualified or aren’t interested in assessing the most concerning risk. Henry Papadatos, executive director of Safer AI, says the problem, even with a public framework, is that voluntary measures are always dependent on a company’s goodwill. “Ideally, we would have good regulation mandating this…because then companies cannot change their mind tomorrow if they have a big PR crisis,” Papadatos told TechCrunch, noting that it’s also a good means of pushing all companies to adhere to the rules, not only the most willing. Not everyone has signed on. So far, Meta, SpaceXAI, and Google DeepMind have not committed to embedding third-party evaluators, though DeepMind CEO Demis Hassabis has proposed a separate industry standards body to independently test frontier models. Google, OpenAI, and Anthropic have also privately been discussing AI safety plans for weeks. Some laws are already forming around the idea of third-party evaluators. California’s SB 53, signed into law last year, requires large frontier AI developers to publish safety frameworks and report critical safety incidents. A new law, SB 813, signed this month, creates a framework for state-recognized “independent verification organizations” with expertise assessing AI risks. In Europe, the EU AI Act requires frontier developers to conduct and document model evaluations and adversarial testing and report serious incidents. The EU AI Office can also conduct its own evaluations and appoint independent experts. For now the law remains less expansive than what Amodei is proposing, leaving frontier labs largely responsible for deciding how much independent scrutiny they will submit to. Papadatos said voluntary self-regulation is better than nothing, but ultimately, companies can’t demand the freedom to control their own safety rules while also asking the public to trust that they’re following them. “You cannot have it both ways, having zero accountability externally, and then say, ‘I’ll just have my own flexible rules,” Papadatos said. X will now let US users trade via Cashtags

X will now let US users trade via Cashtags

TechCrunch | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt X will now allow users to trade from their timelines. The Elon Musk-owned social network on Wednesday launched a feature called Cashtag that will let U.S. users on X place stock trades via participating brokerages. Initial partners in the effort include Interactive Brokers, Moomoo, Gemini, Kraken, and Coinbase. The move takes advantage of what users on X have been doing for years: talking about stocks and, in more recent years, crypto. In 2008, the financial platform Stocktwits even came up with the idea for X-precursor Twitter’s Cashtags, which placed a dollar sign ($) in front of stock ticker symbols to make market conversations easy to find and reference. However, those Cashtags were about tracking the conversations around a particular stock, not actually trading them. Now that X has moved into the financial space with X Money, that’s changed. Now, Cashtags, which turn ticker symbols for stocks, ETFs, and cryptocurrencies into interactive destinations, will give X users the option to trade stocks. “Cashtags close the gap between a ticker on the timeline and the market itself. When you post or tap a ticker, you’re taken right to the asset, where you have seamless access to the live chart, the conversation around it, and now the ability to trade with one of our brokerage partners,” said Mridul Singhai, X Product Engineering lead, in a statement shared with TechCrunch. To use the new feature, users who tap on a supported Cashtag will be able to view the asset’s price chart and related posts on X. They can then tap “Trade” to continue at a participating brokerage. There, they’ll sign in and complete their trade. Users can also tap on a brokerage partner to reach their mobile app or website to log in to their existing account or sign up for a new one, X notes. “With our Cashtag partners, we’re connecting the financial conversation to action. People come to X to discover what’s happening, shape the conversation, and act in real-time on what matters to them,” Monique Pintarelli, SpaceXAI’s head of Global Advertising, said in a statement. “Our Cashtag partners make it possible to move seamlessly from discovery and conversation to a brokerage, without breaking the moment.” However, the feature could spur more market manipulation by the way of AI-controlled bots, spammers, and other bad actors, who could attempt to shift the conversation in one direction or another. But one could argue that this is already happening on X today, even without the direct connection to brokerages. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. How Democrats Are Mobilizing to Counter Potential Election Interference Amid Trump Concerns
2026-09-16

How Democrats Are Mobilizing to Counter Potential Election Interference Amid Trump Concerns

TIME | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Longdale Elementary is a 1st Congressional district polling location in Henrico, Virginia, on Election Day, Aug. 4, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings With the midterm elections less than two months away, Democrats are training thousands of volunteers on how to respond to any potential incidents of voter intimidation at the polls in November. The New Battlefield Project—an initiative that is being coordinated by the Association of State Democratic Committees (ASDC), which operates under the Democratic National Committee (DNC)—involves training at least 10,000 volunteers so that they’ll be equipped to counter interference or intimidation at polling locations across the country if such situations arise, says Jane Kleeb, the chair of the Nebraska Democratic party and president of the ASDC. The on-the-ground team—dubbed the “Democracy Protectors”—will be wearing blue to distinguish themselves, and will be trained in de-escalating conflicts and documenting any potential “bad actors and bad behavior.” Information about such incidents would then be passed along to the state party and the DNC to take action where necessary. Kleeb says the volunteers will also be helpful resources to voters—for instance, they can help direct people to the right polling locations. The trainings, Kleeb adds, are based on constitutional observer trainings that the Minnesota Democratic Party coordinated amid Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) raids in the state. In addition to the Democracy Protectors, the New Battlefield Project includes teams that are focused on encouraging citizens to register to vote and to vote early, as well as on dispelling any misinformation or disinformation spreading online ahead of Election Day. The DNC musters an election protection program every year, and states sometimes train volunteers to perform similar tasks. But this year’s effort is larger in its scope and is being coordinated nationwide. Kleeb says Democrats were spurred to launch the initiative because of speculation and concerns they were hearing from voters, candidates, and elected officials that President Donald Trump could send federal agents to polling places. “About six months ago, state parties really started to put in place a program that we thought would address the threat of Donald Trump and what we think his allies are doing this cycle,” Kleeb says. “We heard Trump and Steve Bannon talking about how they could potentially be sending the National Guard, the military, or ICE agents outside of polling locations.” “We take them very seriously,” she continues. The Justice Department announced last month that its Civil Rights Division would be “monitoring polling sites in Florida and Wyoming for the states’ primary elections to ensure transparency, ballot security, and compliance with federal law.” On Tuesday, FBI Director Kash Patel also indicated that he was not opposed to the idea of dispatching federal agents to voting locations. Separately, Trump has deployed the National Guard to cities across the country, including Los Angeles and Washington, D.C., with the stated goal of helping combat crime, though the data indicates that crime rates in many of those cities have been falling for years. When asked in May if he would send members of the National Guard or ICE officers to the polls, Trump replied, “I’d do anything necessary to make sure we have honest elections.” The troops’ presence, as well as comments from the President and his allies, has raised concerns from some Democrats that the military could be sent to voting locations. Members of the military are prohibited under federal law from being sent to “any place where a general or special election is held,” however. And in August, the chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff told Democratic Sen. Elissa Slotkin in a letter that the “Joint Force has no plans to send Federal military personnel or Federalized members of the National Guard to polling places during the 2026 elections. Likewise, the Joint Force has no plans to use such personnel to seize ballots, voting machines, or other election-related material.” Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said earlier this month that ICE—which has also ramped up enforcement in cities around the U.S. since Trump returned to office—“isn’t there to patrol the polling places.” But he added that “We’re there to enforce specific threats,” a comment that exacerbated fears among Democrats, advocates, and voters that ICE agents may show up at the polls in November. When asked about the possibility of ICE agents being stationed at the polls during the midterms, then-White House Press Secretary Karoline Leavitt said earlier this year that that was “not something [she has] ever heard the President consider.” “I can’t guarantee that an ICE agent won’t be around a polling location in November,” Leavitt said in February. “I mean, that's frankly a very silly hypothetical question. But what I can tell you is I haven’t heard the President discuss any formal plans to put ICE outside of polling locations. It’s a disingenuous question.” Kleeb says that Democrats wanted to be prepared just in case. “Maybe nothing will happen,” Kleeb says. “Maybe they don’t send anybody anywhere. Maybe that’s going to be true. But as the Democratic party … we do not want to wake up the next day after Election Day, saying to ourselves, ‘Damn it! They said that they were going to do this, and we didn’t believe them,’ or ‘We thought that it was just them thumping their chest.’ We’re taking them seriously.” When asked by TIME about the New Battlefield Project, White House spokeswoman Lauren Bis said in a statement that “the only ‘fears’ Americans have about our elections are being fueled by extreme liberal Democrats who refuse to support commonsense legislation that ensures Americans, and Americans only, decide our elections and other basic election security measures like Voter ID.” Bis’ comment appeared to be referring to the SAVE America Act, the restrictive voter ID bill that the President has forcefully championed during his second term that has been met with vocal backlash from Democrats and voting rights advocates. The measure passed the House, but Senate Republicans have said they don’t have the votes required for it to pass the upper chamber. Experts have expressed concerns about potential election interference in the November midterms, in light of recent actions taken by the Trump Administration. “The Trump Administration has been progressively trying to regulate and interfere in elections in ways which are not constitutional,” alleged Pippa Norris, a lecturer in comparative politics at the Harvard Kennedy School and founding director of the Electoral Integrity Project. “Most of his actions haven’t actually succeeded, but they have sown confusion, and therefore there’s been problems because they can undermine public trust in the system.” She cites, for instance, recent reports that the Trump Administration has directed federal agents to comb through voter rolls, which are overseen by the states, in a quest to find non-citizens. Research overwhelmingly shows that voter fraud is extremely rare. Richard Briffault, a professor at the Columbia Law School, says that Trump “has been consistently complaining about fraud and misconduct at the polls, always without evidence.” Trump has repeatedly falsely claimed that the 2020 election was “stolen” from him. Briffault adds that the Trump Administration has dispatched federal officials—such as those from the Justice Department, the National Guard, and ICE—to various places, which could be sparking concerns among local elections officials and voters about potential conflicts at the polls. He also points to the Trump Administration’s effort to impose new restrictions on mail-in ballots—a move that was blocked by the U.S. Supreme Court on Monday, which the President railed against on social media this week. “It is not surprising that many people are nervous that [Trump] might do something to try and disrupt the election if he feels it’s going against him,” Briffault says. “We don’t know what he’s going to do.” “There’s kind of an air of menace that the Administration has generated,” he continues. “Whether they do anything, we won’t know until Election Day or the lead-up to it and after.” Norris says that the practice of sending people to polling places to counter intimidation is often used in other countries during contentious elections. But she cast doubt that the New Battlefield Project was “the most effective thing which the DNC can do,” expressing concern that there isn’t enough time before the November midterms to effectively train volunteers in recognizing and responding to potential issues and that that could raise the possibility of “disputes” or conflicts at polling places, particularly if Republican organizations also dispatch volunteers to those locations. Training for the volunteers started about three weeks ago, according to Kleeb. She adds that Democrats are taking the concern of potential conflict “very seriously;” a critical part of the training, she says, is teaching volunteers how to de-escalate tense situations while making it “very clear” that they should not engage if any violence erupts. Briffault says it’s not clear yet if the initiative will be effective at countering potential intimidation, but that it could foster “a level of comfort” for voters at the polls. He notes, though, that there are so many polling places across the U.S. that it’s not possible for the New Battlefield Project to have a presence at all of them. “I do think it’s important for people to know that there will be other people there looking out to make sure the election goes smoothly and without disruption,” he says. Kleeb says that the volunteers won’t be at every polling place in the country, but each state party will decide the locations that have the most need for them. In Nebraska, for instance, Democrats think the second congressional district is “the most flippable congressional seat” in the state, so the party plans to station volunteers in high-traffic precincts in that area. In some states, the party may look to dispatch volunteers to polling places in areas with high immigrant populations or with many young people who may be voting for the first time, she adds. “Our elections are safe and secure,” Kleeb says. “The Democracy Protectors Program is put in place to ensure that voters have a friendly face outside the polling location to make sure that their vote is counted.”

Both Democrats and Republicans Are Increasingly Worried About AI's Environmental Impact
2026-09-16

Both Democrats and Republicans Are Increasingly Worried About AI's Environmental Impact

TIME | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Drone view of a 49.5 megawatt data center under construction in Vernon, Calif. on July 14, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings As data centers break ground in communities across the country, Americans are growing more concerned about the environmental impacts. That’s according to a new poll from The Associated Press-NORC Center for Public Affairs Research and the Energy Policy Institute at the University of Chicago, which found that about half (53%) of Americans are “extremely” or “very” concerned about AI’s environmental impacts. The number is up from 41% last year. The concern is shared across all party affiliations. More Democrats, Republicans, and Independents said they are “extremely or very” worried about the environmental impacts compared to last year—with 65% of Democrats, 42% of Republicans, and 49% of Independents reporting their concern this year. There are currently 1,287 data centers in operation in the U.S., with more than 2,000 planned projects, according to Clearview, a market intelligence company tracking U.S. power infrastructure and data center development. Virginia and Texas currently have the most data centers in operation, but many new developments are planned in rural areas in the South and Midwest. The growth is driven by the rise in hyperscale data centers, which are larger, more powerful, and often used to train and run large-scale AI models. Read more: Community Backlash to AI Data Centers Is Growing Across the U.S. Despite the boom, Americans seem to be increasingly supportive of measures to regulate growth. About 6 in 10 Americans said they would support limiting the number of new data centers that can be built—including most Democrats and Republicans—the survey found. Democrats seem to be the most concerned by the environmental impacts of data center growth.  About two-thirds say they are “extremely” or “very” concerned about how AI will affect the environment, the poll found, compared to about half in 2025. By contrast, about half of Independents and 4 in 10 Republicans are highly concerned about the environmental impact of AI. Republicans, on the other hand, are more likely to see the benefits of data centers. Yet about 3 in 10 Republicans still believe the centers are “not very” or “not at all” beneficial for improved infrastructure and job creation. A majority (60%) of Americans are “extremely” or “very” concerned about the impact of data centers on electricity prices or the water supply in the communities where they are built, according to the new poll. The concerns are not unfounded. A U.N. report published in June estimated that the water used by AI is expected to greatly expand in the coming years—equaling the needs of 1.3 billion people by 2030 and threatening natural resources for billions around the world. In the U.S. alone, data centers could account for 11.8% of electricity consumption by 2030, according to a 2024 report from the Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory Report. The poll found that Americans are more worried about the environmental impact of AI than other industries with a substantial environmental footprint like the meat industry or air travel. About two-thirds of U.S. adults are in favor of requiring data centers to use clean energy sources, like wind or solar, to supply their electricity needs. Roughly 8 in 10 Democrats support this, along with 56% of Republicans. But in the absence of clean energy alternatives, U.S. data centers are projected to consume more natural gas than most countries by 2035, according to a new report from BloombergNEF. The Trump Administration—which has been undoing climate rules that could help spur clean energy—has strongly supported data center expansion, urging communities to "let Data Reign” in a social media post on Monday. "The only reason that communities throughout the U.S.A. should not want Data Centers is if they want to end up being backwards and poor,” he wrote. “If they want to be successful and rich, with far lower taxes and jobs all over the place, let Data Reign." Americans seem to be less certain. Roughly a quarter of U.S. adults surveyed said data centers are “extremely” or “very” beneficial when it comes to technological advancement, and only about 2 in 10 say the same about job creation. Many U.S. adults say data centers are “somewhat” beneficial or aren’t sure, while about one quarter say they’re “not very” or “not at all” beneficial for technological advancement, and around a third don’t see any benefit for job creation. (Some research has shown that, while data center construction can yield a number of temporary jobs, facilities tend to employ fewer permanent employees.) The survey results come as a growing number of cities and states have been placing moratoriums on data center growth until the impacts are better understood. As of September 2026, 313 moratoriums have been enacted, affecting 44 states and more than 200 cities, counties, and localities, according to the Urban Institute. Many believe it's essential to pause data center growth now—before the number of data centers across the country explodes. “We’re undertaking a massive experiment in real time, and I don’t think there’ll be any putting the milk back in the bottle,” Michael Greenstone, who directs the University of Chicago institute that collaborated on the survey, told the AP. “Change is scary, and I think some of that is being reflected here.”

The Surprising Health Benefits of Reading a Book
2026-09-16

The Surprising Health Benefits of Reading a Book

TIME | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings When was the last time you sat down with a good book? Over the last couple of decades, the number of Americans reading for pleasure has been steadily dropping, with one large study finding that only 16% of people read for pleasure. Trends elsewhere are similarly in decline; in the U.K., the percentage of people who read regularly dropped 8% over the last decade or so. This might seem like an unimportant, if slightly depressing, side effect of people’s attention being fixed on screens. But a new review published in Psychological Medicine suggests that reading for pleasure—sitting down and focusing on a book you actually look forward to cracking open—can have significant impacts on your brain and wellbeing. Reading has an effect from a very young age, says Barbara Sahakian, an emeritus professor of clinical neuropsychology at the University of Cambridge in the U.K. and an author of the new paper. In one study of about 10,000 children in the U.S., she and her colleagues found that kids who started to read for pleasure early in life were better adjusted later, regardless of their socioeconomic background. “They had better cognition, they had reduced symptoms of mental-health problems, they had better attention,” she says. They also spent less time on screens, slept longer, and performed better in school. But reading for pleasure shouldn’t stop in childhood. Studies of adult reading habits show benefits as well. In the review, the authors note that readers often mention in surveys that they feel less lonely after they read. “‘It made me feel more connected to others. It made me understand other people's point of view better, and gave me more empathy.’ That comes up a lot in surveys,” Sahakian says. There are neuroimaging studies to back up that finding, she continues. “When you read fiction, particularly stories that are rich in social content, it activates areas in the brain that we know are involved in social cognition, areas that always come up when you're doing studies of theory of mind or understanding things from other people's point of view,” she says. Surveys indicate a link to stress reduction as well. “With reducing stress, it seems to happen really quickly,” Sahakian says. “If you get engrossed in a book, then your mind and your attention are really focused on reading and the content, which means it takes your attention away from other things. You don't start ruminating about problems that you have…you forget all of that. So it's a little bit like mindfulness in that regard, because the attention is so focused.” Some of the research Sahakian finds most exciting focuses on older people’s reading habits. She refers to one study in Taiwan that followed people over age 64 for 14 years.  “People who just read once a week had a significantly reduced risk of cognitive decline over that 14-year period, and importantly, it didn't relate to educational levels,” she says. “What is so good about reading is that you get these benefits regardless of socio-economic background or educational level. It will improve your brain. It will improve your cognition. It will improve your well-being,” she says. If it’s been a while since you’ve read for pleasure, there’s no need to start with War and Peace. Reading works best when it relates to something you are actually interested in, says Sahakian. Try a magazine or book related to a hobby or special interest to start. There’s not as much research about the body’s response to  listening to audiobooks, so the trends are less clear. One study found that students who studied written material did better on a quiz later than students who listened to the material on a podcast, suggesting that taking facts in through the ears can make it harder to absorb them. Perhaps more importantly, when it comes to reading for pleasure, people often listen to audiobooks while they are doing something else—like driving, gardening, or doing the dishes. That suggests, says Sahakian, that they aren’t producing the same feeling of absorption that reading text does.

Saudi Arabia Calls Mecca a ‘Red Line’ After Alleged Houthi Drone Attack
